I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that. I mean, every man’s got his place, right? My father used to say a shop runs right only when everybody does their part, and that’s true whether you’re on the floor or helping at church or loading up boxes for a move.\n\nNow, do I get frustrated when folks don’t see what’s right in front of them? Sure. Anybody would. Like if you’ve got a grinder set wrong, you don’t just stand there and admire it, you fix it before somebody gets hurt or ruins the piece. But that doesn’t mean I’m sitting around thinking I’m more important than everybody else. I just notice things, I guess. Maybe more than some people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're more capable of handling difficult tasks than most people?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, Doctor. Difficult tasks… that depends on the task, doesn’t it? I mean, if you’re talking about something in the shop, something with the equipment, I’ve handled plenty over the years. You learn to keep your head down and do it right.\n\nBut “more capable than most people,” I don’t know. My neighbor used to say he could fix anything with duct tape and a prayer, and then his gutter came clean off in a storm. So people think they can handle things until they can’t. I guess I try to handle what’s put in front of me. Whether that makes me more capable than others… I couldn’t say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that you are superior to others?\n\nPatient: Superior? No, I don’t think so. That’s a pretty tall word.\n\nI mean, I’ve known men who thought they were superior—walk into a room like everybody else was furniture. I never had much patience for that. There was a fellow years ago at the plant, wouldn’t listen to anybody because he had some certificate from a training course, and then he nearly jammed up the whole line because he didn’t want to hear from the older guys. That’s not being superior, that’s being foolish.\n\nNow, I might think I’m right about certain things when I’ve got experience with them. If I’ve done a job a hundred times and someone’s doing it wrong, I’ll say so. But superior to people? No… I don’t think of myself that way. I’m just trying to get through things like everybody else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often expect special treatment or automatic deference from others?\n\nPatient: Special treatment? No… I don’t think I expect that. I mean, there are little courtesies, sure—hold the door if a man’s carrying something heavy, don’t talk over somebody when they’re trying to explain a thing. That’s just manners, not special treatment.\n\nNow, automatic deference… that makes me think of the old foreman we had, wanted everyone to hop to just because he cleared his throat. I never liked that. Respect ought to be earned, same as a clean edge on a wheel—you don’t just get it because you walked in wearing the right shirt.\n\nI suppose at my age I notice when younger folks don’t listen, but that’s probably every generation saying the same thing. My father complained about us, I complained about my boys, and now the kids all have those little screens in their hands half the time. But no, I don’t go around expecting people to bow or treat me like I’m somebody special.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your opinions or contributions are more valuable than those of others?\n\nPatient: More valuable? Well… I wouldn’t put it that way. I mean, if we’re talking about something I know—like machinery, or how a batch is supposed to look when it’s mixed right—then sure, I’d expect my opinion to carry some weight. You don’t spend years around grinders and polishers without learning a thing or two.\n\nBut then again, I remember this one kid at the shop, barely old enough to shave, and he noticed a sound in one of the machines that the rest of us had been ignoring. Turned out he was right. So you can’t just assume the old guy always knows best.\n\nI don’t know that my contributions are more valuable than other people’s. Depends on what we’re talking about, I guess. Everybody sees a different piece of the thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you deserve more recognition or reward for your efforts than you actually receive?\n\nPatient: Recognition… well, I don’t know. I suppose everybody likes to be thanked when they’ve worked hard. That’s just human, isn’t it?\n\nI remember at the plant, sometimes you’d get a whole run finished clean, no waste, no stoppages, and the only time anybody came around was when something went wrong. That used to bother the men more than they’d admit. But then again, that’s work. Most places don’t hand you a parade for doing what you were hired to do.\n\nAs for me… I don’t sit around thinking I’m owed some big reward. Maybe now and then I’ve thought, “Well, somebody could’ve noticed that,” but I wouldn’t say it’s often. It’s hard to measure those things. Some people need a plaque on the wall; I was usually satisfied if the machine ran right and nobody got hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming about achieving great things or being recognized as an important figure?\n\nPatient: Daydreaming about being important… I don’t know. Not in any grand way, I don’t think. I mean, a fellow might imagine once in a while that somebody finally notices he did a job right, or that the boss says, “That was good work,” but that’s not exactly standing on a stage with people clapping.\n\nWhen I was younger, I used to think maybe I’d run a shop someday, or invent some better guard for one of those machines—there was one grinder, I remember, had a shield that never sat quite right, and you’d get dust where dust had no business being. Funny the things that stick in your mind.\n\nBut… being an important figure? No, I wouldn’t say I sit around picturing that. Mostly my mind wanders to ordinary things—old jobs, family, whether I left something half-done. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by daydreaming.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special talent or ability that sets you apart from others?\n\nPatient: A special talent… I don’t know that I’d call it special. I was good with machinery, I suppose—could hear when something was running a little off, or tell by the texture if a mix wasn’t right yet. But a lot of men could do that if they’d been around it long enough.\n\nIt’s funny, though, because people think those jobs are all muscle, but there’s a feel to it. Like baking bread almost—my mother used to say dough tells you what it needs, and I never understood that until I was standing there with a batch that wasn’t blending right. Same sort of thing, I guess.\n\nBut as far as something that sets me apart… no, I’m not sure. I’ve noticed things here and there. I wouldn’t say I’ve got some special ability beyond other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about your accomplishments and feel they're more significant than what others have achieved?\n\nPatient: More significant than other people’s… I don’t really think of it that way. I mean, accomplishments are funny things. One man builds a house, another keeps a line running for thirty years, another raises three kids and nobody gives him a medal. Who’s to say which is bigger?\n\nI’ve thought about things I did, sure—jobs that went well, problems I figured out. There was one time we had a mixer that kept throwing off the whole batch, and everyone thought it was the material, but it was a worn part making the timing just a little wrong. Little thing, but it mattered. Though now that I say it, that’s not exactly something you put in a history book.\n\nSo no, I don’t sit there thinking my accomplishments are above everybody else’s. I mostly just remember bits and pieces, and sometimes they lead to other memories.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a higher social status than most people you meet?\n\nPatient: Higher social status… no, I don’t think so. I mean, most people I meet, I don’t really rank them like that. Some wear nicer shoes, some know how to talk to bank people, some can fix a motor without looking at the manual—depends what room you’re standing in, I guess.\n\nI worked with men who barely finished school but could keep a machine alive that should’ve been scrap ten years before. And then you’d get someone with a tie come through the plant and not know which end of the line was which. But that doesn’t mean I was above him, exactly. Just different kinds of knowing.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I feel higher up than most people. I mostly just try to get through the conversation without putting my foot in my mouth.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking you would be more successful or accomplished if others would just listen to and follow your advice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… lately I do catch myself thinking that. Like if everybody would just listen for once, things would move the way they’re supposed to. I’ll have a plan in my head, and it seems clear as daylight to me, and then somebody asks one little question—my wife, or my boy—and I’ll snap, like they’re blocking the whole road.\n\nAnd it isn’t always like that. Sometimes I’m joking around, feeling big, like I’m ten feet tall and could straighten the whole mess out if people would just get on board. Then next minute I’m irritated over nothing. It’s like… either come along with the plan or step aside, you know? Which sounds harsher when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like things need to be done perfectly, even if it takes a lot of time and effort?\n\nPatient: Perfectly? I don’t know if I’d say perfectly. That’s a big word. I like things done right, sure, especially if you’re working with machinery—because if a mix is off or something isn’t lined up, then the whole batch can go bad, and then everybody’s standing around pointing at everybody else.\n\nBut in the house? I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll fuss with something longer than I meant to, like a drawer that doesn’t slide right, and then I remember my father used to keep screws in coffee cans—old Maxwell House cans, lined up on a shelf—and he always knew which one had what. I never could get mine like that. Mine end up all mixed together, and then you spend twenty minutes looking for one little washer.\n\nSo… I guess I like things to be decent. I don’t know about perfect. I haven’t really thought of myself that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your accomplishments and abilities make you stand out from others in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Stand out… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s got something they can do better than the next fellow, right? I could run certain equipment pretty well, knew by the sound when a mix was wrong before anybody had to tell me, but that’s just from years of standing there with the dust in your shirt and your ears ringing.\n\nI wouldn’t call that “significant,” exactly. My uncle used to say if you can fix a thing with your hands, you’ll always have supper, but then he also bought three lawnmowers that didn’t run, so I don’t know how much wisdom there was in that.\n\nSo… no, I guess I haven’t really thought of my accomplishments like they make me stand out. I did my work, raised my family as best I could, same as a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special gift or talent that most people don't have?\n\nPatient: A special gift? No… I don’t think I’d put it like that. I mean, I’ve been good with my hands at times, could hear when a machine wasn’t running right, like I said, but that’s not magic or anything. That’s just being around it long enough.\n\nMy brother-in-law used to say he had a “gift” for finding deals, but mostly he came home with things nobody else wanted—broken radios, bent tools, boxes of tile that didn’t match. So when people say special gift, I’m never sure what they mean.\n\nI guess… no, I haven’t really noticed anything like that in myself. Not most people don’t have, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that your opinions or actions have a significant impact on the people around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “significant impact” sounds pretty big. I mean, people hear you or they don’t. My wife used to say I’d mutter about something being done wrong and then go fix it myself anyway, so maybe that had some effect around the house, but that’s just living with somebody long enough, isn’t it?\n\nAt work, if I said a machine sounded off, sometimes the younger guys would stop and check it, but that was more because they didn’t want a mess on their hands. There was one fellow, Eddie, he’d never clean the hopper right, and then the next batch would—well, anyway, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t sit around thinking my opinions change everybody. My neighbor used to say he could fix anything with duct tape and a prayer, and then his gutter came clean off in a storm. So people think they can handle things until they can’t. I never liked that. My father complained about us, I complained about my boys, and now the kids all have those little screens in their hands half the time. But no, I don’t go around expecting people to bow or treat me like I’m somebody special.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your opinions or contributions are more valuable than those of others?\n\nPatient: More valuable? Well… I wouldn’t put it that way. I mean, if we’re talking about something I know—like machinery, or how a batch is supposed to look when it’s mixed right—then sure, I’d expect my opinion to carry some weight. You don’t spend years around grinders and polishers without learning a thing or two.\n\nBut then again, I remember this one kid at the shop, barely old enough to shave, and he noticed a sound in one of the machines that the rest of us had been ignoring. That used to bother the men more than they’d admit. But then again, that’s work. I’m not sure I’d know if they did.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you believe you are more deserving of respect than others simply because of who you are?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / crushing_grinding_polishing_mixing_or_blending_worker / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More deserving just because I’m me? No, I don’t think so. Respect’s one of those things… you give it unless somebody shows you they’re not worth it, I suppose.

My father was funny about that—he’d tip his hat to the bank manager and the fellow sweeping the sidewalk the same way, but then he’d come home and complain about both of them over supper. So I never quite knew what lesson he meant by it.

I guess I don’t figure I’m owed more than the next person. I like being treated decently, same as anybody. But not because I’m somehow above them, no.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.0, F34.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here mostly because everybody’s been on me saying I’m doing too much. My family, people at church, even a couple fellas at work act like I ought to slow down—but honestly, to me it feels like I’m the only one with enough get-up-and-go to get things handled.\n\nWe’ve got this move going on, and the new garage needs to be rebuilt right, not half-done like some folks do it. Then there’s church stuff that needs organizing, and at work these younger guys… I mean, they don’t teach them the way they used to. Somebody’s got to show them how the job ought to be done. So I guess they think that’s a problem, but I don’t see how having drive is a bad thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your ideas and ways of doing things are better than others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that exactly. I mean, “better” sounds a little puffed up, doesn’t it? I just… I’ve been around long enough to know when something’s being done the wrong way.\n\nLike with grinding work, you can tell real quick who’s paying attention and who’s just trying to get through the shift. Same with church committees—Lord, you get six people in a room and somehow nobody wants to make a decision. So I’ll say what I think, sure. But whether that means I think I’m better than everybody… I don’t know. I just know I see things that need fixing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're destined for something great or that you have a special role to play in the world?\n\nPatient: Destined for something great? I don’t know about all that. That sounds like something a man would say if he was running for office or had his picture on a billboard.\n\nI mean, I do believe the Lord gives everybody work to do, and if you see something that needs doing, you ought not sit around waiting for somebody else. Like at church, last month they couldn’t even get the chairs set up right for the supper, and somehow I’m the one who notices these things. But “special role in the world”… I don’t know. I’ve got responsibilities, same as anybody. I just try to step up where other folks drag their feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling more important than others around you?\n\nPatient: More important? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that. I mean, every man’s got his place, right? My father used to say a shop runs right only when everybody does their part, and that’s true whether you’re on the floor or helping at church or loading up boxes for a move.\n\nNow, do I get frustrated when folks don’t see what’s right in front of them? Sure. Anybody would. Like if you’ve got a grinder set wrong, you don’t just stand there and admire it, you fix it before somebody gets hurt or ruins the piece. But that doesn’t mean I’m sitting around thinking I’m more important than everybody else. I just notice things, I guess. Maybe more than some people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're more capable of handling difficult tasks than most people?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, Doctor. Difficult tasks… that depends on the task, doesn’t it? I mean, if you’re talking about something in the shop, something with the equipment, I’ve handled plenty over the years. You learn to keep your head down and do it right.\n\nBut “more capable than most people,” I don’t know. My neighbor used to say he could fix anything with duct tape and a prayer, and then his gutter came clean off in a storm. So people think they can handle things until they can’t. I guess I try to handle what’s put in front of me. Whether that makes me more capable than others… I couldn’t say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that you are superior to others?\n\nPatient: Superior? No, I don’t think so. That’s a pretty tall word.\n\nI mean, I’ve known men who thought they were superior—walk into a room like everybody else was furniture. I never had much patience for that. There was a fellow years ago at the plant, wouldn’t listen to anybody because he had some certificate from a training course, and then he nearly jammed up the whole line because he didn’t want to hear from the older guys. That’s not being superior, that’s being foolish.\n\nNow, I might think I’m right about certain things when I’ve got experience with them. If I’ve done a job a hundred times and someone’s doing it wrong, I’ll say so. But superior to people? No… I don’t think of myself that way. I’m just trying to get through things like everybody else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often expect special treatment or automatic deference from others?\n\nPatient: Special treatment? No… I don’t think I expect that. I mean, there are little courtesies, sure—hold the door if a man’s carrying something heavy, don’t talk over somebody when they’re trying to explain a thing. That’s just manners, not special treatment.\n\nNow, automatic deference… that makes me think of the old foreman we had, wanted everyone to hop to just because he cleared his throat. I never liked that. Respect ought to be earned, same as a clean edge on a wheel—you don’t just get it because you walked in wearing the right shirt.\n\nI suppose at my age I notice when younger folks don’t listen, but that’s probably every generation saying the same thing. My father complained about us, I complained about my boys, and now the kids all have those little screens in their hands half the time. But no, I don’t go around expecting people to bow or treat me like I’m somebody special.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your opinions or contributions are more valuable than those of others?\n\nPatient: More valuable? Well… I wouldn’t put it that way. I mean, if we’re talking about something I know—like machinery, or how a batch is supposed to look when it’s mixed right—then sure, I’d expect my opinion to carry some weight. You don’t spend years around grinders and polishers without learning a thing or two.\n\nBut then again, I remember this one kid at the shop, barely old enough to shave, and he noticed a sound in one of the machines that the rest of us had been ignoring. Turned out he was right. So you can’t just assume the old guy always knows best.\n\nI don’t know that my contributions are more valuable than other people’s. Depends on what we’re talking about, I guess. Everybody sees a different piece of the thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you deserve more recognition or reward for your efforts than you actually receive?\n\nPatient: Recognition… well, I don’t know. I suppose everybody likes to be thanked when they’ve worked hard. That’s just human, isn’t it?\n\nI remember at the plant, sometimes you’d get a whole run finished clean, no waste, no stoppages, and the only time anybody came around was when something went wrong. That used to bother the men more than they’d admit. But then again, that’s work. Most places don’t hand you a parade for doing what you were hired to do.\n\nAs for me… I don’t sit around thinking I’m owed some big reward. Maybe now and then I’ve thought, “Well, somebody could’ve noticed that,” but I wouldn’t say it’s often. It’s hard to measure those things. Some people need a plaque on the wall; I was usually satisfied if the machine ran right and nobody got hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming about achieving great things or being recognized as an important figure?\n\nPatient: Daydreaming about being important… I don’t know. Not in any grand way, I don’t think. I mean, a fellow might imagine once in a while that somebody finally notices he did a job right, or that the boss says, “That was good work,” but that’s not exactly standing on a stage with people clapping.\n\nWhen I was younger, I used to think maybe I’d run a shop someday, or invent some better guard for one of those machines—there was one grinder, I remember, had a shield that never sat quite right, and you’d get dust where dust had no business being. Funny the things that stick in your mind.\n\nBut… being an important figure? No, I wouldn’t say I sit around picturing that. Mostly my mind wanders to ordinary things—old jobs, family, whether I left something half-done. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by daydreaming.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special talent or ability that sets you apart from others?\n\nPatient: A special talent… I don’t know that I’d call it special. I was good with machinery, I suppose—could hear when something was running a little off, or tell by the texture if a mix wasn’t right yet. But a lot of men could do that if they’d been around it long enough.\n\nIt’s funny, though, because people think those jobs are all muscle, but there’s a feel to it. Like baking bread almost—my mother used to say dough tells you what it needs, and I never understood that until I was standing there with a batch that wasn’t blending right. Same sort of thing, I guess.\n\nBut as far as something that sets me apart… no, I’m not sure. I’ve noticed things here and there. I wouldn’t say I’ve got some special ability beyond other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about your accomplishments and feel they're more significant than what others have achieved?\n\nPatient: More significant than other people’s… I don’t really think of it that way. I mean, accomplishments are funny things. One man builds a house, another keeps a line running for thirty years, another raises three kids and nobody gives him a medal. Who’s to say which is bigger?\n\nI’ve thought about things I did, sure—jobs that went well, problems I figured out. There was one time we had a mixer that kept throwing off the whole batch, and everyone thought it was the material, but it was a worn part making the timing just a little wrong. Little thing, but it mattered. Though now that I say it, that’s not exactly something you put in a history book.\n\nSo no, I don’t sit there thinking my accomplishments are above everybody else’s. I mostly just remember bits and pieces, and sometimes they lead to other memories.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a higher social status than most people you meet?\n\nPatient: Higher social status… no, I don’t think so. I mean, most people I meet, I don’t really rank them like that. Some wear nicer shoes, some know how to talk to bank people, some can fix a motor without looking at the manual—depends what room you’re standing in, I guess.\n\nI worked with men who barely finished school but could keep a machine alive that should’ve been scrap ten years before. And then you’d get someone with a tie come through the plant and not know which end of the line was which. But that doesn’t mean I was above him, exactly. Just different kinds of knowing.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I feel higher up than most people. I mostly just try to get through the conversation without putting my foot in my mouth.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking you would be more successful or accomplished if others would just listen to and follow your advice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… lately I do catch myself thinking that. Like if everybody would just listen for once, things would move the way they’re supposed to. I’ll have a plan in my head, and it seems clear as daylight to me, and then somebody asks one little question—my wife, or my boy—and I’ll snap, like they’re blocking the whole road.\n\nAnd it isn’t always like that. Sometimes I’m joking around, feeling big, like I’m ten feet tall and could straighten the whole mess out if people would just get on board. Then next minute I’m irritated over nothing. It’s like… either come along with the plan or step aside, you know? Which sounds harsher when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like things need to be done perfectly, even if it takes a lot of time and effort?\n\nPatient: Perfectly? I don’t know if I’d say perfectly. That’s a big word. I like things done right, sure, especially if you’re working with machinery—because if a mix is off or something isn’t lined up, then the whole batch can go bad, and then everybody’s standing around pointing at everybody else.\n\nBut in the house? I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll fuss with something longer than I meant to, like a drawer that doesn’t slide right, and then I remember my father used to keep screws in coffee cans—old Maxwell House cans, lined up on a shelf—and he always knew which one had what. I never could get mine like that. Mine end up all mixed together, and then you spend twenty minutes looking for one little washer.\n\nSo… I guess I like things to be decent. I don’t know about perfect. I haven’t really thought of myself that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your accomplishments and abilities make you stand out from others in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Stand out… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s got something they can do better than the next fellow, right? I could run certain equipment pretty well, knew by the sound when a mix was wrong before anybody had to tell me, but that’s just from years of standing there with the dust in your shirt and your ears ringing.\n\nI wouldn’t call that “significant,” exactly. My uncle used to say if you can fix a thing with your hands, you’ll always have supper, but then he also bought three lawnmowers that didn’t run, so I don’t know how much wisdom there was in that.\n\nSo… no, I guess I haven’t really thought of my accomplishments like they make me stand out. I did my work, raised my family as best I could, same as a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special gift or talent that most people don't have?\n\nPatient: A special gift? No… I don’t think I’d put it like that. I mean, I’ve been good with my hands at times, could hear when a machine wasn’t running right, like I said, but that’s not magic or anything. That’s just being around it long enough.\n\nMy brother-in-law used to say he had a “gift” for finding deals, but mostly he came home with things nobody else wanted—broken radios, bent tools, boxes of tile that didn’t match. So when people say special gift, I’m never sure what they mean.\n\nI guess… no, I haven’t really noticed anything like that in myself. Not most people don’t have, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that your opinions or actions have a significant impact on the people around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “significant impact” sounds pretty big. I mean, people hear you or they don’t. My wife used to say I’d mutter about something being done wrong and then go fix it myself anyway, so maybe that had some effect around the house, but that’s just living with somebody long enough, isn’t it?\n\nAt work, if I said a machine sounded off, sometimes the younger guys would stop and check it, but that was more because they didn’t want a mess on their hands. There was one fellow, Eddie, he’d never clean the hopper right, and then the next batch would—well, anyway, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t sit around thinking my opinions change everybody. I’m not sure I’d know if they did.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are more deserving of respect than others simply because of who you are?\n\nPatient: More deserving just because I’m me? No, I don’t think so. Respect’s one of those things… you give it unless somebody shows you they’re not worth it, I suppose.\n\nMy father was funny about that—he’d tip his hat to the bank manager and the fellow sweeping the sidewalk the same way, but then he’d come home and complain about both of them over supper. So I never quite knew what lesson he meant by it.\n\nI guess I don’t figure I’m owed more than the next person. I like being treated decently, same as anybody. But not because I’m somehow above them, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F60.0", "F34.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

There was one time we had a mixer that kept throwing off the whole batch, and everyone thought it was the material, but it was a worn part making the timing just a little wrong. Little thing, but it mattered. Though now that I say it, that’s not exactly something you put in a history book.\n\nSo no, I don’t sit there thinking my accomplishments are above everybody else’s. I mostly just remember bits and pieces, and sometimes they lead to other memories.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a higher social status than most people you meet?\n\nPatient: Higher social status… no, I don’t think so. I mean, most people I meet, I don’t really rank them like that. Some wear nicer shoes, some know how to talk to bank people, some can fix a motor without looking at the manual—depends what room you’re standing in, I guess.\n\nI worked with men who barely finished school but could keep a machine alive that should’ve been scrap ten years before. And then you’d get someone with a tie come through the plant and not know which end of the line was which. But that doesn’t mean I was above him, exactly. Just different kinds of knowing.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I feel higher up than most people. I mostly just try to get through the conversation without putting my foot in my mouth.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking you would be more successful or accomplished if others would just listen to and follow your advice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… lately I do catch myself thinking that. Like if everybody would just listen for once, things would move the way they’re supposed to. I’ll have a plan in my head, and it seems clear as daylight to me, and then somebody asks one little question—my wife, or my boy—and I’ll snap, like they’re blocking the whole road.\n\nAnd it isn’t always like that. Sometimes I’m joking around, feeling big, like I’m ten feet tall and could straighten the whole mess out if people would just get on board. Then next minute I’m irritated over nothing. It’s like… either come along with the plan or step aside, you know? Which sounds harsher when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like things need to be done perfectly, even if it takes a lot of time and effort?\n\nPatient: Perfectly? I don’t know if I’d say perfectly. That’s a big word. I like things done right, sure, especially if you’re working with machinery—because if a mix is off or something isn’t lined up, then the whole batch can go bad, and then everybody’s standing around pointing at everybody else.\n\nBut in the house? I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll fuss with something longer than I meant to, like a drawer that doesn’t slide right, and then I remember my father used to keep screws in coffee cans—old Maxwell House cans, lined up on a shelf—and he always knew which one had what. I never could get mine like that. Mine end up all mixed together, and then you spend twenty minutes looking for one little washer.\n\nSo… I guess I like things to be decent. I don’t know about perfect. I haven’t really thought of myself that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your accomplishments and abilities make you stand out from others in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Stand out… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s got something they can do better than the next fellow, right? I could run certain equipment pretty well, knew by the sound when a mix was wrong before anybody had to tell me, but that’s just from years of standing there with the dust in your shirt and your ears ringing.\n\nI wouldn’t call that “significant,” exactly. My uncle used to say if you can fix a thing with your hands, you’ll always have supper, but then he also bought three lawnmowers that didn’t run, so I don’t know how much wisdom there was in that.\n\nSo… no, I guess I haven’t really thought of my accomplishments like they make me stand out. I did my work, raised my family as best I could, same as a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special gift or talent that most people don't have?\n\nPatient: A special gift? No… I don’t think I’d put it like that. I mean, I’ve been good with my hands at times, could hear when a machine wasn’t running right, like I said, but that’s not magic or anything. That’s just being around it long enough.\n\nMy brother-in-law used to say he had a “gift” for finding deals, but mostly he came home with things nobody else wanted—broken radios, bent tools, boxes of tile that didn’t match. So when people say special gift, I’m never sure what they mean.\n\nI guess… no, I haven’t really noticed anything like that in myself. Not most people don’t have, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that your opinions or actions have a significant impact on the people around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “significant impact” sounds pretty big. I mean, people hear you or they don’t. My wife used to say I’d mutter about something being done wrong and then go fix it myself anyway, so maybe that had some effect around the house, but that’s just living with somebody long enough, isn’t it?\n\nAt work, if I said a machine sounded off, sometimes the younger guys would stop and check it, but that was more because they didn’t want a mess on their hands. There was one fellow, Eddie, he’d never clean the hopper right, and then the next batch would—well, anyway, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t sit around thinking my opinions change everybody. I’m not sure I’d know if they did.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are more deserving of respect than others simply because of who you are?\n\nPatient: More deserving just because I’m me? No, I don’t think so. Respect’s one of those things… you give it unless somebody shows you they’re not worth it, I suppose.\n\nMy father was funny about that—he’d tip his hat to the bank manager and the fellow sweeping the sidewalk the same way, but then he’d come home and complain about both of them over supper. So I never quite knew what lesson he meant by it.\n\nI guess I don’t figure I’m owed more than the next person. I like being treated decently, same as anybody. But not because I’m somehow above them, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s more recent, I’d say. Not my whole life or anything like that. It’s been… about a month now, since we had to start leaving the old house.

That house had its problems, sure—old wiring, steps that always creaked in the same places, and the back door never shut right unless you lifted it a little. I kept meaning to fix that hinge properly. But once people started saying we had to get out, everything just started feeling… tight. Like everybody was in the way, or not understanding.

So no, I wouldn’t say it’s been years and years. More like since all this business with the house started. A month, maybe.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.0, F34.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here mostly because everybody’s been on me saying I’m doing too much. My family, people at church, even a couple fellas at work act like I ought to slow down—but honestly, to me it feels like I’m the only one with enough get-up-and-go to get things handled.\n\nWe’ve got this move going on, and the new garage needs to be rebuilt right, not half-done like some folks do it. Then there’s church stuff that needs organizing, and at work these younger guys… I mean, they don’t teach them the way they used to. Somebody’s got to show them how the job ought to be done. So I guess they think that’s a problem, but I don’t see how having drive is a bad thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your ideas and ways of doing things are better than others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that exactly. I mean, “better” sounds a little puffed up, doesn’t it? I just… I’ve been around long enough to know when something’s being done the wrong way.\n\nLike with grinding work, you can tell real quick who’s paying attention and who’s just trying to get through the shift. Same with church committees—Lord, you get six people in a room and somehow nobody wants to make a decision. So I’ll say what I think, sure. But whether that means I think I’m better than everybody… I don’t know. I just know I see things that need fixing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're destined for something great or that you have a special role to play in the world?\n\nPatient: Destined for something great? I don’t know about all that. That sounds like something a man would say if he was running for office or had his picture on a billboard.\n\nI mean, I do believe the Lord gives everybody work to do, and if you see something that needs doing, you ought not sit around waiting for somebody else. I’ve got responsibilities, same as anybody. I just try to step up where other folks drag their feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling more important than others around you?\n\nPatient: More important? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say it like that. I mean, every man’s got his place, right? My father used to say a shop runs right only when everybody does their part, and that’s true whether you’re on the floor or helping at church or loading up boxes for a move.\n\nNow, do I get frustrated when folks don’t see what’s right in front of them? Sure. Anybody would. Like if you’ve got a grinder set wrong, you don’t just stand there and admire it, you fix it before somebody gets hurt or ruins the piece. But that doesn’t mean I’m sitting around thinking I’m more important than everybody else. I just notice things, I guess. Maybe more than some people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're more capable of handling difficult tasks than most people?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, Doctor. Difficult tasks… that depends on the task, doesn’t it? I mean, if you’re talking about something in the shop, something with the equipment, I’ve handled plenty over the years. You learn to keep your head down and do it right.\n\nBut “more capable than most people,” I don’t know. My neighbor used to say he could fix anything with duct tape and a prayer, and then his gutter came clean off in a storm. So people think they can handle things until they can’t. I guess I try to handle what’s put in front of me. Whether that makes me more capable than others… I couldn’t say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think that you are superior to others?\n\nPatient: Superior? No, I don’t think so. That’s a pretty tall word.\n\nI mean, I’ve known men who thought they were superior—walk into a room like everybody else was furniture. I never had much patience for that. There was a fellow years ago at the plant, wouldn’t listen to anybody because he had some certificate from a training course, and then he nearly jammed up the whole line because he didn’t want to hear from the older guys. That’s not being superior, that’s being foolish.\n\nNow, I might think I’m right about certain things when I’ve got experience with them. If I’ve done a job a hundred times and someone’s doing it wrong, I’ll say so. But superior to people? No… I don’t think of myself that way. I’m just trying to get through things like everybody else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often expect special treatment or automatic deference from others?\n\nPatient: Special treatment? No… I don’t think I expect that. I mean, there are little courtesies, sure—hold the door if a man’s carrying something heavy, don’t talk over somebody when they’re trying to explain a thing. That’s just manners, not special treatment.\n\nNow, automatic deference… that makes me think of the old foreman we had, wanted everyone to hop to just because he cleared his throat. I never liked that. Respect ought to be earned, same as a clean edge on a wheel—you don’t just get it because you walked in wearing the right shirt.\n\nI suppose at my age I notice when younger folks don’t listen, but that’s probably every generation saying the same thing. My father complained about us, I complained about my boys, and now the kids all have those little screens in their hands half the time. But no, I don’t go around expecting people to bow or treat me like I’m somebody special.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your opinions or contributions are more valuable than those of others?\n\nPatient: More valuable? Well… I wouldn’t put it that way. I mean, if we’re talking about something I know—like machinery, or how a batch is supposed to look when it’s mixed right—then sure, I’d expect my opinion to carry some weight. You don’t spend years around grinders and polishers without learning a thing or two.\n\nBut then again, I remember this one kid at the shop, barely old enough to shave, and he noticed a sound in one of the machines that the rest of us had been ignoring. Turned out he was right. So you can’t just assume the old guy always knows best.\n\nI don’t know that my contributions are more valuable than other people’s. Depends on what we’re talking about, I guess. Everybody sees a different piece of the thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you deserve more recognition or reward for your efforts than you actually receive?\n\nPatient: Recognition… well, I don’t know. I suppose everybody likes to be thanked when they’ve worked hard. That’s just human, isn’t it?\n\nI remember at the plant, sometimes you’d get a whole run finished clean, no waste, no stoppages, and the only time anybody came around was when something went wrong. That used to bother the men more than they’d admit. But then again, that’s work. Most places don’t hand you a parade for doing what you were hired to do.\n\nAs for me… I don’t sit around thinking I’m owed some big reward. Maybe now and then I’ve thought, “Well, somebody could’ve noticed that,” but I wouldn’t say it’s often. It’s hard to measure those things. Some people need a plaque on the wall; I was usually satisfied if the machine ran right and nobody got hurt.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming about achieving great things or being recognized as an important figure?\n\nPatient: Daydreaming about being important… I don’t know. Not in any grand way, I don’t think. I mean, a fellow might imagine once in a while that somebody finally notices he did a job right, or that the boss says, “That was good work,” but that’s not exactly standing on a stage with people clapping.\n\nWhen I was younger, I used to think maybe I’d run a shop someday, or invent some better guard for one of those machines—there was one grinder, I remember, had a shield that never sat quite right, and you’d get dust where dust had no business being. Funny the things that stick in your mind.\n\nBut… being an important figure? No, I wouldn’t say I sit around picturing that. Mostly my mind wanders to ordinary things—old jobs, family, whether I left something half-done. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by daydreaming.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special talent or ability that sets you apart from others?\n\nPatient: A special talent… I don’t know that I’d call it special. I was good with machinery, I suppose—could hear when something was running a little off, or tell by the texture if a mix wasn’t right yet. But a lot of men could do that if they’d been around it long enough.\n\nIt’s funny, though, because people think those jobs are all muscle, but there’s a feel to it. Like baking bread almost—my mother used to say dough tells you what it needs, and I never understood that until I was standing there with a batch that wasn’t blending right. Same sort of thing, I guess.\n\nBut as far as something that sets me apart… no, I’m not sure. I’ve noticed things here and there. I wouldn’t say I’ve got some special ability beyond other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about your accomplishments and feel they're more significant than what others have achieved?\n\nPatient: More significant than other people’s… I don’t really think of it that way. I mean, accomplishments are funny things. One man builds a house, another keeps a line running for thirty years, another raises three kids and nobody gives him a medal. Who’s to say which is bigger?\n\nI’ve thought about things I did, sure—jobs that went well, problems I figured out. There was one time we had a mixer that kept throwing off the whole batch, and everyone thought it was the material, but it was a worn part making the timing just a little wrong. Little thing, but it mattered. Though now that I say it, that’s not exactly something you put in a history book.\n\nSo no, I don’t sit there thinking my accomplishments are above everybody else’s. I mostly just remember bits and pieces, and sometimes they lead to other memories.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a higher social status than most people you meet?\n\nPatient: Higher social status… no, I don’t think so. I mean, most people I meet, I don’t really rank them like that. Some wear nicer shoes, some know how to talk to bank people, some can fix a motor without looking at the manual—depends what room you’re standing in, I guess.\n\nI worked with men who barely finished school but could keep a machine alive that should’ve been scrap ten years before. And then you’d get someone with a tie come through the plant and not know which end of the line was which. But that doesn’t mean I was above him, exactly. Just different kinds of knowing.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I feel higher up than most people. I mostly just try to get through the conversation without putting my foot in my mouth.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking you would be more successful or accomplished if others would just listen to and follow your advice?\n\nPatient: Yeah… lately I do catch myself thinking that. Like if everybody would just listen for once, things would move the way they’re supposed to. I’ll have a plan in my head, and it seems clear as daylight to me, and then somebody asks one little question—my wife, or my boy—and I’ll snap, like they’re blocking the whole road.\n\nAnd it isn’t always like that. Sometimes I’m joking around, feeling big, like I’m ten feet tall and could straighten the whole mess out if people would just get on board. Then next minute I’m irritated over nothing. It’s like… either come along with the plan or step aside, you know? Which sounds harsher when I say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like things need to be done perfectly, even if it takes a lot of time and effort?\n\nPatient: Perfectly? I don’t know if I’d say perfectly. That’s a big word. I like things done right, sure, especially if you’re working with machinery—because if a mix is off or something isn’t lined up, then the whole batch can go bad, and then everybody’s standing around pointing at everybody else.\n\nBut in the house? I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll fuss with something longer than I meant to, like a drawer that doesn’t slide right, and then I remember my father used to keep screws in coffee cans—old Maxwell House cans, lined up on a shelf—and he always knew which one had what. I never could get mine like that. Mine end up all mixed together, and then you spend twenty minutes looking for one little washer.\n\nSo… I guess I like things to be decent. I don’t know about perfect. I haven’t really thought of myself that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your accomplishments and abilities make you stand out from others in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Stand out… I don’t know. I mean, everybody’s got something they can do better than the next fellow, right? I could run certain equipment pretty well, knew by the sound when a mix was wrong before anybody had to tell me, but that’s just from years of standing there with the dust in your shirt and your ears ringing.\n\nI wouldn’t call that “significant,” exactly. My uncle used to say if you can fix a thing with your hands, you’ll always have supper, but then he also bought three lawnmowers that didn’t run, so I don’t know how much wisdom there was in that.\n\nSo… no, I guess I haven’t really thought of my accomplishments like they make me stand out. I did my work, raised my family as best I could, same as a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special gift or talent that most people don't have?\n\nPatient: A special gift? No… I don’t think I’d put it like that. I mean, I’ve been good with my hands at times, could hear when a machine wasn’t running right, like I said, but that’s not magic or anything. That’s just being around it long enough.\n\nMy brother-in-law used to say he had a “gift” for finding deals, but mostly he came home with things nobody else wanted—broken radios, bent tools, boxes of tile that didn’t match. So when people say special gift, I’m never sure what they mean.\n\nI guess… no, I haven’t really noticed anything like that in myself. Not most people don’t have, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that your opinions or actions have a significant impact on the people around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “significant impact” sounds pretty big. I mean, people hear you or they don’t. My wife used to say I’d mutter about something being done wrong and then go fix it myself anyway, so maybe that had some effect around the house, but that’s just living with somebody long enough, isn’t it?\n\nAt work, if I said a machine sounded off, sometimes the younger guys would stop and check it, but that was more because they didn’t want a mess on their hands. There was one fellow, Eddie, he’d never clean the hopper right, and then the next batch would—well, anyway, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t sit around thinking my opinions change everybody. I’m not sure I’d know if they did.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are more deserving of respect than others simply because of who you are?\n\nPatient: More deserving just because I’m me? No, I don’t think so. Respect’s one of those things… you give it unless somebody shows you they’re not worth it, I suppose.\n\nMy father was funny about that—he’d tip his hat to the bank manager and the fellow sweeping the sidewalk the same way, but then he’d come home and complain about both of them over supper. So I never quite knew what lesson he meant by it.\n\nI guess I don’t figure I’m owed more than the next person. I like being treated decently, same as anybody. But not because I’m somehow above them, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you consistently felt this driven and frustrated with others over a long period, or has this been a recent development?\n\nPatient: It’s more recent, I’d say. Not my whole life or anything like that. It’s been… about a month now, since we had to start leaving the old house.\n\nThat house had its problems, sure—old wiring, steps that always creaked in the same places, and the back door never shut right unless you lifted it a little. I kept meaning to fix that hinge properly. But once people started saying we had to get out, everything just started feeling… tight. Like everybody was in the way, or not understanding.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s been years and years. More like since all this business with the house started. A month, maybe.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
